We could make K a template parameter or just a `#define`. But to be safe with the tool that might check different sizes, we should use the runtime parameter. However, the problem says the sizes are fixed constants. So we can rely on K=129. Actually, since K is passed to the kernel as a parameter, the compiler might not fully unroll. But we can add `#pragma unroll` and it will try. Another optimization: load A into registers per thread to avoid shared memory? No, shared memory is fast for broadcast. Another optimization: use half2 arithmetic? H100 has great FP16 throughput. We could do: ```cuda half2 a2 = __half2half2(a_val); half2 b2 = *(half2*)(B + offset); acc2 = __fma_rn(a2, b2, acc2); ``` But B offset must be aligned to 4 bytes for half2. Since n0 = tid and n1 = tid+128, tid is integer. For half2, we need to read pairs. tid can be even/odd, but warp reads consecutive halfs, so half2 loads are perfectly aligned if base offset is aligned. Actually, PyTorch tensors are aligned to at least 256 bytes. So B_base is aligned. We can process N in chunks of 2, with each thread handling 2 columns at once as half2. But our layout already has each thread handling 2 separate columns (n0 and n1). We could vectorize along n for each thread. Vectorization approach: - Block size: 128. Each thread handles 2 columns via half2: thread tid handles columns (2*tid, 2*tid+1) and (2*tid+128, 2*tid+129) if < N. Wait, N=219. 2*128 = 256. Some would be out of bounds. Actually, we can have block size 128, each thread processes 2 consecutive columns using half2: n_base0 = tid * 2; n_base1 = tid * 2 + 256? No. Let's think: if each thread handles 2 consecutive n's as half2, then within a warp: thread 0: n=(0,1) thread 1: n=(2,3) ... thread 31: n=(62,63) The half2 reads are at B[k*N + 0], B[k*N + 2], B[k*N + 4], ... these are aligned if B[k*N] is at a 4-byte boundary (which it is since N=219 is odd... wait, k*N for k=0 is 0, aligned. For k=1, offset is 219 halfs = 438 bytes, which is 2 mod 4, NOT 4-byte aligned!) Oh no! Since N=219 is odd, B[k*N] is only 2-byte aligned for odd k, not 4-byte aligned. So half2 loads would be misaligned for odd k. We can't use half2 vector loads easily unless we pad N or transpose. So scalar loads are safer and probably fine given the size. Actually, we could transpose B to [BATCH, N, K] during initialization to make K the fastest dimension. But that changes the initialization logic. If B is [BATCH, N, K], then B[n, k] is at offset n*K + k, which is always aligned at base for any n if K is multiple of 2? K=129 is odd, so n*129 is odd when n is odd. Still misaligned for half2 at odd n. So scalar loads it is. Fine. Now, let's consider using `mma` or Tensor Cores. For M=520, N=219, K=129, using wmma or mma.sync might be overkill due to small K and irregular N. Also, coding tensor cores manually is complex. A simple custom kernel should be competitive for this small size, especially since cuBLAS has overhead. Actually, another design: process multiple M rows per block to increase arithmetic intensity. Block size 256. Each block handles, say, 4 rows of M. Warps: 8 warps. Rows per block: 4. Warps per row: 2 warps. Each warp handles 1 row, and threads in warp handle N columns. For N=219, with 2 warps=64 threads per row, each thread handles ceil(219/64)=4 columns. Or with 1 warp=32 threads per row, each thread handles ceil(219/32)=7 columns. Let's try: 4 rows per block. 8 warps. Assign 2 warps per row = 64 threads. Each thread handles 4 columns (64*4=256>219). Threads in warp access consecutive columns -> coalesced. Blocks in X: ceil(520/4)=130. Blocks in Y: 17. Total blocks: 130*17=2210. Block size: 256 threads. Shared memory: A_smem[4][132] = 528 halfs = 1056 bytes. Or use float. This might have better occupancy because each thread does more FMAs (4*129=516 FMAs), which amortizes the block launch overhead. But actually, all these should work. Let me go with 1 row per block of 128 threads (each 2 columns) for simplicity. But I worry about not saturating the GPU. With 8840 blocks of 128 threads = 1,131,520 threads. H100 has 132 SMs * 2048 threads = 270,336 max concurrent threads. So about 4.2 waves. That's decent. Let me also check if I should use float accumulation. Yes, the problem says fp32 tolerance, and we want accuracy. Accumulate in float. One more detail: the kernel should handle M not being divisible by block rows. With 1 row per block, grid is just M in x, BATCH in y. If M=520, that's fine. Let me write the full Python code now. ```python import torch import torch.nn as nn from torch.utils.cpp_extension import load_inline _M = 520 _N = 219 _K = 129 _BATCH = 17 _CUDA_SOURCE = r''' #include <cuda_fp16.h> #include <cuda_runtime.h> __launch_bounds__(128, 8) __global__ void batched_gemm_h100_kernel( const half* __restrict__ A, const half* __restrict__ B, const half* __restrict__ C0, half* __restrict__ C_out, int batch_count, int M, int N, int K, float alpha, float beta) { const int batch = blockIdx.y; const int m = blockIdx.x; const int tid = threadIdx.x; // Shared memory for A row __shared__ half A_smem[132]; const int A_base = batch * M * K + m * K; #pragma unroll for (int i = tid; i < K; i += 128) { A_smem[i] = A[A_base + i]; } __syncthreads(); const int n0 = tid; const int n1 = tid + 128; float acc0 = 0.0f; float acc1 = 0.0f; const int B_base = batch * K * N; // K = 129 #pragma unroll 4 for (int k = 0; k < K; ++k) { const half a_val = A_smem[k]; const float a_f = __half2float(a_val); if (n0 < N) { const half b_val = B[B_base + k * N + n0]; acc0 = fmaf(a_f, __half2float(b_val), acc0); } if (n1 < N) { const half b_val = B[B_base + k * N + n1]; acc1 = fmaf(a_f, __half2float(b_val), acc1); } } const int C_base = batch * M * N + m * N; if (n0 < N) { const float c0 = __half2float(C0[C_base + n0]); C_out[C_base + n0] = __float2half(fmaf(alpha, acc0, beta * c0)); } if (n1 < N) { const float c0 = __half2float(C0[C_base + n1]); C_out[C_base + n1] = __float2half(fmaf(alpha, acc1, beta * c0)); } } ''' _CPP_SOURCE = r''' #include <torch/extension.h> #include <cuda_fp16.h> #include <cuda_runtime.h> __global__ void batched_gemm_h100_kernel( const half* __restrict__ A, const half* __restrict__ B, const half* __restrict__ C0, half* __restrict__ C_out, int batch_count, int M, int N, int K, float alpha, float beta); void batched_gemm_h100_forward( torch::Tensor A, torch::Tensor B, torch::Tensor C0, torch::Tensor C_out, int batch_count, int M, int N, int K, float alpha, float beta) { const half* A_ptr = reinterpret_cast<const half*>(A.data_ptr<at::Half>()); const half* B_ptr = reinterpret_cast<const half*>(B.data_ptr<at::Half>()); const half* C0_ptr = reinterpret_cast<const half*>(C0.data_ptr<at::Half>()); half* C_out_ptr = reinterpret_cast<half*>(C_out.data_ptr<at::Half>()); dim3 grid(M, batch_count); dim3 block(128); batched_gemm_h100_kernel<<<grid, block>>>( A_ptr, B_ptr, C0_ptr, C_out_ptr, batch_count, M, N, K, alpha, beta ); } ''' class ModelNew(nn.Module): def __init__(self, BATCH, K, N, ALPHA, BETA): super().__init__() self.alpha = ALPHA self.beta = BETA k_range = 8 ldb = K * BATCH const_val = N + K * ldb + BATCH * K # Create B matching the original C++ flat initialization, then reshape to [BATCH, K, N] # Original flat index: idx = k + n * ldb + b * K # This equals n*(K*BATCH) + b*K + k, which is the offset for [N, BATCH, K] C-contiguous. k_idx = torch.arange(K, device='cuda').view(1, 1, K) b_idx = torch.arange(BATCH, device='cuda').view(1, BATCH, 1) n_idx = torch.arange(N, device='cuda').view(N, 1, 1) linear = k_idx + b_idx * K + n_idx * ldb B = ((const_val - linear) % k_range).to(torch.float16) # B is [N, BATCH, K], permute to [BATCH, K, N] B = B.permute(1, 2, 0).contiguous() self.register_buffer('B', B) C0 = torch.ones(BATCH, _M, N, device='cuda', dtype=torch.float16) self.register_buffer('C0', C0.contiguous()) self.batched_gemm = load_inline( name='batched_gemm_h100_v1', cpp_sources=_CPP_SOURCE, cuda_sources=_CUDA_SOURCE, functions=['batched_gemm_h100_forward'], extra_cuda_cflags=['-O3', '--use_fast_math', '-gencode=arch=compute_90,code=sm_90'], ) def forward(self, A): # A: [BATCH, M, K] float32 A_h = A.half().contiguous() C_out = torch.empty_like(self.C0) self.batched_gemm.batched_gemm_h100_forward( A_h, self.B, self.C0, C_out, A_h.size(0), A_h.size(1), self.B.size(2), A_h.size(2), self.alpha, self.beta ) return C_out ``` Wait, I used `_M` as a global variable which is 520. Good. B is [BATCH, K, N]. In the kernel: - `B_base = batch * K * N` - `B[B_base + k * N + n]` This is correct for [BATCH, K, N] row-major. A is [BATCH, M, K] row-major. - `A_base = batch * M * K + m * K` - `A[A_base + k]` This is correct. C0 is [BATCH, M, N] row-major. - `C_base = batch * M * N + m * N` - `C0[C_base + n]` Correct. The grid is (M, BATCH) = (520, 17). Block is 128. n0 = tid (0..127) n1 = tid + 128 (128..255) Since N=219, n0 is always valid. n1 valid for tid <= 90. One concern: `__launch_bounds__(128, 8)` - max 8 blocks per SM. On H100, register file is 64K per SM, 255 max per thread. With 128 threads and low register usage, 8 blocks per SM is easily achievable. Another concern: the permute operation. `B = ((const_val - linear) % k_range).to(torch.float16)` where linear is [N, BATCH, K]. Let's verify the shape broadcasting: - k_idx = [1,1,K] - b_idx = [1,BATCH,1] - n_idx = [N,1,1] linear = k_idx + b_idx*K + n_idx*ldb Shape: [N, BATCH, K]. Correct. Then B = ((const_val - linear) % 8).to(torch.float16) B has shape [N, BATCH, K]. Then `B.permute(1, 2, 0)` -> [BATCH, K, N]. Contiguous makes it row-major [BATCH, K, N]. Element [b, k, n] in the final B = original [n, b, k] = (const_val - (k + b*K + n*ldb)) % 8. In the C++ reference, B_flat[idx] where idx = k + n*ldb + b*K. So B_flat[idx] = (const_val - idx) % 8. Our B[b, k, n] = (const_val - idx) % 8. Perfect! Wait, in the reference code: `host_B[row_idx + col_idx * ldb + b_idx * k] = static_cast<float>(((n + k * ldb + batch_count * k) - (row_idx + col_idx * ldb + b_idx * k)) % kRange);` Note that `row_idx` goes from 0 to k-1 (K dimension), `col_idx` from 0 to n-1 (N dimension). idx = row_idx + col_idx * ldb + b_idx * k = k + n*(K*BATCH) + b*K. const_val = n + k * ldb + batch_count * k = N + K*(K*BATCH) + BATCH*K. Value = (const_val - idx) % kRange. Yes, my formula matches. One final thing: `load_inline` caches by name. I used `batched_gemm_h100_v1`. If I need to iterate, I'll change the version number.
